  • NFR-116 (Deployment/environment): All assets and fonts are served from the installation's own origin; no third-party CDN is required, so air-gapped deployments render fully.

F-9: Global AI Chat Companion

  • NFR-121 (Performance): First response token p95 < 2 s; complete grounded answer p95 < 6 s for typical context sizes.
  • NFR-122 (Scalability): ≥ 200 concurrent chat sessions per installation with context scoped to the objects relevant to the operator's current view.
  • NFR-123 (Availability): When the AI backend is unavailable, the companion degrades to deterministic help and search with a clear indicator, never presenting ungrounded output as authoritative.
  • NFR-124 (Accuracy/grounding): Every factual claim about pipeline state cites the authoritative object it came from; if state cannot be resolved the companion refuses rather than inventing state or commands.
  • NFR-125 (Security): The companion operates strictly within the operator's authorization scope and resists prompt-injection attempts that would escalate to unauthorized data access or command execution.
  • NFR-126 (Safety): The companion drafts commands only; execution requires preview and confirmation through the manual command surface (F-6) and is never self-executed.
  • NFR-127 (Privacy): Conversation content is handled per the installation's documented policy; tenant data is not used for model training without explicit opt-in.
  • NFR-128 (Data residency): Prompts, retrieved context and transcripts are processed and stored within the configured residency region; model routing respects residency constraints.
  • NFR-129 (Auditability): Command drafts, cited objects and refusal events are logged with the requesting operator and session.
  • NFR-130 (Deployment/environment): Supports a locally hosted or self-hosted model endpoint so the companion functions in fully air-gapped deployments.

F-10: Voice Companion

  • NFR-136 (Performance): Partial speech-to-text transcript latency < 500 ms; end-of-speech to start of spoken answer p95 < 3 s.
  • NFR-137 (Performance): Barge-in (operator interrupting spoken output) stops playback within < 300 ms.
  • NFR-138 (Scalability): ≥ 50 concurrent voice sessions per installation.
  • NFR-139 (Availability): When voice services are unavailable, the companion transparently degrades to the text experience of F-9 with a clear indicator.
  • NFR-140 (Accessibility): All voice-driven functionality has an equivalent keyboard/text path; spoken output is accompanied by captions/transcript.
  • NFR-141 (Security): Microphone access requires explicit per-session consent, shows a persistent capture indicator, and never records in the background.
  • NFR-142 (Privacy): Audio retention is off by default; when enabled it is bounded and documented, transcripts follow policy, and redaction controls are available.
  • NFR-143 (Safety): Voice-originated commands are drafted only and never executed by voice alone; destructive actions require visual, non-voice confirmation.
  • NFR-144 (Internationalization): Speech recognition and synthesis support the documented locales, with locale-appropriate pronunciation and number/date rendering.
  • NFR-145 (Data residency): Audio and transcripts are processed in the residency region; any speech provider used must be region-pinned and documented.
  • NFR-146 (Deployment/environment): Supports on-device or locally hosted STT/TTS so voice functions in air-gapped and offline conditions where configured.
  • NFR-147 (Auditability): Voice-originated command drafts, hands-free HIL announcements and accept/reject outcomes are logged with operator and session identity.

F-11: Mobile Dashboard Companion

  • NFR-151 (Performance): Cold start < 3 s on a mid-tier device; list scrolling maintains 60 fps on supported hardware.
  • NFR-152 (Performance): Push notification delivery p95 < 10 s from the originating pipeline event.
  • NFR-153 (Scalability): Browsing ≥ 10,000 objects via paging without memory growth; offline cache bounded (target ≤ 50 MB) with documented eviction.
  • NFR-154 (Availability): Read-only access to last-known state is available offline, with a clear offline indicator and graceful reconnect/resync.
  • NFR-155 (Security): Device-bound sessions, biometric unlock, remote wipe, encrypted local storage, TLS pinning where supported; no domain data in unencrypted storage or logs.
  • NFR-156 (Security): Sensitive actions require re-authentication; the app executes no commands itself and delegates every command to F-6 for preview, confirmation and audit.
  • NFR-157 (Privacy): Notification content can be redacted for lock-screen safety; notification payloads contain no credentials or sensitive artifact contents.
  • NFR-158 (Accessibility): Touch targets ≥ 44×44 px, full screen-reader support, dynamic type, and sufficient contrast in both light and dark themes.
  • NFR-159 (Data residency): Local caches respect residency policy; no cross-region synchronization of cached state.
  • NFR-160 (Deployment/environment): Distributes via the installation's own channel or enterprise MDM and functions on a local network in air-gapped deployments.
  • NFR-161 (Compatibility): Supported on documented minimum iOS/Android versions, on both phones and tablets, in portrait and landscape.

F-12: Test Management

  • NFR-166 (Performance): Test definition reads p95 < 300 ms; result ingestion p95 < 500 ms; plan-level gate verdict computed within ≤ 5 s of the last required result.
  • NFR-167 (Scalability): ≥ 100,000 test cases, ≥ 1,000,000 retained results, and ≥ 500 concurrent test executions per installation.
  • NFR-168 (Availability/safety): When the test subsystem is degraded, a governed quality gate fails closed with an explicit verdict and never reports an implicit pass.
  • NFR-169 (Determinism): A given artifact plus environment plus settings reproduces the same outcome; flakiness is quantified and surfaced rather than silently retried to green.
  • NFR-170 (Security): Test code and artifacts execute in a sandboxed, least-privilege environment; secrets are injected at runtime and never embedded in definitions or results; pipeline credentials are never exposed to tests.
  • NFR-171 (Auditability): Definitions, executions, results, evidence, waivers and approvals are recorded immutably with actor and timestamp.
  • NFR-172 (Data residency): Test results and evidence artifacts are stored in the configured residency region.
  • NFR-173 (Deployment/environment): Execution agents run air-gapped and can be hosted on-premises with no external runner dependency.
  • NFR-174 (Retention): Result and evidence retention is configurable, defaults to 400 days, and applies secure destruction on expiry.
  • NFR-175 (Integrity): Quality-gate verdicts are hash-chained or signed; a past verdict cannot be retroactively altered without producing a new, separately audited record.

F-13: API-First Access

  • NFR-181 (Performance): Read endpoints p95 < 300 ms and write endpoints p95 < 500 ms at rated load.
  • NFR-182 (Scalability): Sustains ≥ 200 req/s with burst capacity to ≥ 1,000 req/s per installation; list endpoints require pagination and reject unbounded result sets.
  • NFR-183 (Availability): API availability ≥ 99.9% monthly; published versions remain available for their documented deprecation window.
  • NFR-184 (Security): Client authentication via OIDC/OAuth2 with scope-based authorization, short-lived access tokens, rotating refresh credentials, and immediate revocation on credential compromise.
  • NFR-185 (Security): Per-client rate limiting and quotas are enforced; a uniform error envelope is returned that never leaks internal implementation details or stack traces.
  • NFR-186 (Reliability): All writes accept idempotency keys; optimistic concurrency via ETag/If-Match; long-running operations follow an asynchronous job pattern with pollable status.
  • NFR-187 (Contract integrity): A machine-readable contract is published and verified in CI against live behaviour; an undocumented or unverified endpoint is treated as a defect; breaking changes require a new version with ≥ 2 minor releases of deprecation notice.
  • NFR-188 (Auditability): API activity is audited per client credential with actor, scope, target and outcome; audit records are immutable.
  • NFR-189 (Data residency): Requests and resulting data are processed and stored in the client's configured residency region; no third-party egress without explicit opt-in.
  • NFR-190 (Deployment/environment): The API is available with equivalent contracts in cloud, self-hosted and air-gapped profiles and has no mandatory external dependency.
  • NFR-191 (Streaming): Telemetry streams support reconnect and resume-from-cursor so a dropped connection does not lose events or duplicate them into the consumer's state.
  • NFR-192 (Webhooks/events): Delivery is at-least-once with signed payloads, exponential backoff retry, a dead-letter path, and operator-triggered replay.

F-14: API Reuse and Extension Analysis

  • NFR-196 (Performance): Inventory snapshot build ≤ 60 s; gap/reuse query p95 < 2 s.
  • NFR-197 (Scalability): Analyzes ≥ 5,000 API operations against ≥ 10,000 demand entries without breaching NFR-196.
  • NFR-198 (Availability): The analysis layer is read-only and cannot block API publication; its unavailability never degrades F-13.
  • NFR-199 (Accuracy): Every reuse/extension classification references the exact contract version and evidence; no finding is emitted without a resolvable citation.
  • NFR-200 (Security): The layer holds read-only scope over contracts and has no ability to mutate, deprecate or publish an API on another feature's behalf.
  • NFR-201 (Auditability): Review decisions and published reuse guidance carry an immutable record of inputs, reviewer and outcome.
  • NFR-202 (Freshness): Inventory snapshots refresh within ≤ 15 minutes of a contract change, or on explicit operator demand.
  • NFR-203 (Data residency): Inventory snapshots and demand registry data are stored in the configured residency region.
  • NFR-204 (Deployment/environment): Operates in all deployment profiles, including air-gapped, using only locally available contract sources.

F-15: Deployment Options

  • NFR-211 (Performance): Provisioning of a standard profile completes within ≤ 30 minutes; upgrade-plan generation < 60 s.
  • NFR-212 (Scalability): Documented sizing profiles span single-node through multi-region HA, each with published capacity limits and supported component counts.
  • NFR-213 (Availability): The HA profile delivers ≥ 99.95% availability with tested failover; each profile publishes explicit RPO/RTO guarantees.
  • NFR-214 (Recovery): Backup/restore is automated and drill-verified; standard profile RPO ≤ 15 minutes and RTO ≤ 4 hours.
  • NFR-215 (Security): Secrets are sourced from an external secret manager or KMS with no plaintext at rest; hardened profiles are CIS-aligned; container images are signed and accompanied by an SBOM.
  • NFR-216 (Security): TLS is enforced on all external interfaces and mTLS where supported internally; identity federation is supported for operator authentication.
  • NFR-217 (Data residency): A deployment is pinned to the operator's chosen region; no data movement across regions occurs without explicit configuration and documented consent.
  • NFR-218 (Deployment/environment): Cloud, self-hosted and air-gapped/offline delivery are all supported, with air-gapped installs delivered as signed bundles containing every dependency.
  • NFR-219 (Configuration integrity): Continuous drift detection produces a parity report; promotion between environments is gated by approval and reproducible from a recorded configuration revision.
  • NFR-220 (Observability): Metrics, logs, traces and health/parity endpoints are wired by default for every profile, requiring no additional instrumentation.
  • NFR-221 (Upgrade/rollback): Upgrades are reversible; rollback completes within ≤ 30 minutes with no data loss against the stated RPO.
  • NFR-222 (Cost transparency): A cost estimate is available for each profile before provisioning, based on documented sizing assumptions.
  • NFR-223 (Decommissioning): Decommissioning performs secure data destruction and issues a verifiable destruction certificate.
  • NFR-224 (Entitlement): Entitlement binding is enforced at runtime, with offline validation supported for air-gapped installations.

F-16: Voice Reference Alignment

  • NFR-226 (Performance): Alignment of a 5-minute source sample completes within ≤ 3 minutes; speaker-embedding extraction ≤ 30 s per sample.
  • NFR-227 (Scalability): Supports ≥ 10,000 reference profiles and ≥ 100 concurrent alignment jobs per installation without breaching NFR-226.
  • NFR-228 (Availability): Alignment jobs are queued and retried; a failure is isolated to the affected reference and never blocks other profiles, approvals or the Voice Companion's existing bindings.
  • NFR-229 (Quality determinism): The same samples plus the same recorded alignment-settings version produce the same alignment score; settings versions are recorded on every verdict.
  • NFR-230 (Security): Access to reference profiles and source audio is role-gated; source audio cannot be downloaded or exported without an authorized, audited action.
  • NFR-231 (Privacy/consent): A reference cannot be created without a recorded consent/licence basis; retention and deletion are enforced, and deletion removes derived embeddings as well as source audio.
  • NFR-232 (Data residency): Source audio, embeddings and derived profiles are stored in the configured residency region with no egress to external services.
  • NFR-233 (Auditability): Ingestion, preprocessing, alignment, scoring, approval, binding and retirement all produce immutable audit records.
  • NFR-234 (Deployment/environment): Batch alignment runs air-gapped with no external service dependency; all models and tooling ship with the installation.
  • NFR-235 (Storage integrity): Reference artifacts are hash/signature verified on ingest and read; reference-profile versions are immutable once approved.
  • NFR-236 (Retention): Retired references are retained per policy and then securely destroyed, with the destruction recorded in the audit trail.

User Stories

F-1: Central Pipeline Control Plane

  • US-1: As an operator, I want to create and register a pipeline project, so that I can manage its lifecycle from a single control plane.
  • Acceptance Criteria: A project is persisted with name, description, owner, portfolio membership, status, pipeline binding, and configuration revision; it appears in the project registry immediately after creation.
  • US-2: As an operator, I want to search, filter, and inspect the project registry, so that I can find and review projects efficiently.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Registry supports list, detail, search, filter, and pagination; p95 read latency meets NFR-1; archived projects are distinguishable from active ones.
  • US-3: As an operator, I want to update, archive, restore, and permanently delete projects, so that I can manage the full project lifecycle.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Each lifecycle action is persisted, authorized, and auditable; archive hides the project from default views but preserves it; permanent delete requires explicit confirmation and is blocked when dependent runs exist per policy.
  • US-4: As a portfolio manager, I want to group projects into portfolios and view aggregate state, so that I can monitor related projects together.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Portfolios can be created and managed; projects can be assigned and unassigned; roll-ups show run counts by status, gate backlog, failure counts, and last activity.
  • US-5: As a platform administrator, I want to register pipeline definitions and pin immutable versions to projects, so that runs are reproducible.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Pipeline definitions are composed of ordered stages; each project is pinned to a specific immutable version; explicit upgrade is allowed without mutating historical runs.
  • US-6: As an operator, I want to start, pause, resume, cancel, and retry pipeline runs, so that I can control execution.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Commands apply to the whole run or a specified stage; each command is durably persisted before acknowledgement; acknowledgements meet NFR-2.
  • US-7: As an operator, I want stage orchestration to follow the registered 32-stage model, so that runs progress correctly and predictably.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Stage order is resolved from the pinned pipeline version; entry conditions are evaluated; stage outcomes are captured; transitions occur only when conditions are satisfied.
  • US-8: As an auditor, I want every control-plane command and lifecycle change recorded in an audit trail, so that I can trace who did what and when.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Audit records include actor, timestamp, target object, command/action, previous and new state where applicable, and correlation identifiers.

F-2: Project Creation and Model Tier Management

  • US-16: As an operator, I want a guided, multi-step project creation wizard, so that I can create a new pipeline project without missing required information.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Wizard is launched from the control plane; steps are resumable across sessions; incomplete drafts are preserved and clearly marked.
  • US-17: As an operator, I want to capture the seed idea and optional structured goal fields, so that the project starts with clear intent.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Free-form idea text is stored on the project draft; optional fields for goal, target users, and success signals are validated and attached.
  • US-18: As an operator, I want to enter and validate project metadata, so that the project is identifiable and governed correctly.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Name, description, owner, tags, and visibility are collected; required fields are validated; duplicate or invalid names are rejected with actionable errors.
  • US-19: As an operator, I want to assign the new project to a portfolio during creation, so that it is grouped immediately.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Operator can assign zero or one portfolio; inline portfolio creation is available when none exists; assignment is persisted with the project.
  • US-20: As an operator, I want to bind a pipeline definition and pinned version at creation, so that the project uses the correct pipeline.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Operator can select a definition and version; if none is selected, the platform default is bound; the binding is immutable per run history.
  • US-21: As an operator, I want to select a predefined model tier, so that I can control cost and capability trade-offs.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Economical, balanced, and frontier tiers are selectable; the selected tier is resolved and stored with the project; unavailable tiers are clearly indicated.
  • US-22: As an operator, I want to customize a model tier, so that I can tune model and provider choices for a project.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Custom tier can override models/providers within allowed bounds; validation prevents unsupported combinations; changes are versioned or applied to the project draft as configured.
  • US-23: As an operator, I want to create a new tier from the list of LLM models and providers, so that I can define reusable tiers for future projects.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Tier creation uses the registered model/provider list; required fields are validated; the new tier becomes selectable in future project creation flows subject to authorization.

F-3: E2E Pipeline Initiation and Monitoring

  • US-31: As an operator, I want to initiate a run from an existing project, so that the pipeline starts executing.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Run initiation returns a stable run identifier immediately; optional idempotency key, run label, and permitted starting-stage override are accepted.
  • US-32: As an operator, I want pre-flight readiness validation before a run starts, so that I can fix missing prerequisites first.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Validation checks seed idea, required metadata, resolved model tier, bound pipeline definition/version, and gate policy; failures return an itemized, actionable readiness report.
  • US-33: As an operator, I want the run plan to be materialized from the pinned pipeline version, so that execution is deterministic.
  • Acceptance Criteria: The plan expands the pinned version into ordered stages; the plan is immutable for the life of the run; historical runs are not affected by later pipeline changes.
  • US-34: As an operator, I want live progress and telemetry streaming, so that I can monitor the run as it happens.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Stage transitions, status changes, logs/metrics, and gate waits are streamed or pollable; data is consistent with the authoritative run record.
  • US-35: As an operator, I want human-in-the-loop gates to pause the run for decisions, so that humans can intervene at critical points.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Gates block advancement until a decision is recorded; pending gates are visible; decisions are attributed, timestamped, and auditable.
  • US-36: As an operator, I want a complete, auditable record of everything that happened during the run, so that I can investigate and prove outcomes.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Run record includes stage history, gate decisions, commands, timestamps, actors, and outcomes; record is immutable after run completion except for approved corrections.
  • US-37: As an operator, I want to retry failed stages or resume after a pause, so that transient failures can be recovered.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Retry/resume respects stage entry conditions and run state; repeated commands are idempotent where applicable; outcomes are recorded.
  • US-38: As an operator, I want to cancel a run, so that I can stop unwanted or unsafe execution.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Cancellation stops further stage dispatch; in-flight work is handled per policy; cancellation is recorded as an auditable command.

F-4: Portfolio Management

  • US-46: As a portfolio manager, I want to create and govern portfolios, so that I can organize projects into durable groups.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Portfolio has name, description, owner, membership rules, and authorization scope; lifecycle actions are auditable.
  • US-47: As a portfolio manager, I want to add and remove member projects, so that the portfolio always reflects the intended set.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Membership changes are validated, authorized, and persisted; removal does not delete the project; membership history is auditable.
  • US-48: As a portfolio manager, I want aggregated roll-ups across member projects, so that I can see portfolio health at a glance.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Roll-ups show run counts by status, gate backlog, failure counts, capacity pressure, and last activity; data is always current relative to authoritative sources.
  • US-49: As a portfolio manager, I want to issue scoped bulk operations over member projects and runs, so that I can act at portfolio scale.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Bulk operations respect F-3 run initiation/pre-flight rules; each child action is auditable; failures are reported per target without silently skipping.
  • US-50: As a portfolio manager, I want authorization scoped to each portfolio, so that only permitted users can view or manage it.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Role-based access is enforced for read, write, bulk operations, and reporting; unauthorized attempts are denied and audited.
  • US-51: As a portfolio manager, I want reporting on portfolio state, so that I can share status with stakeholders.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Reports include roll-up metrics, trends, and drill-down links to source projects/runs; export is available where authorized.

F-5: Multi-Project Runs

  • US-61: As an operator, I want to create a multi-project run group, so that I can coordinate the same or compatible pipeline across many projects.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Parent run group has name, owner, optional portfolio context, scope definition, execution mode, failure policy, and shared parameters; it is durably persisted.
  • US-62: As an operator, I want to define project scope and execution mode, so that the group targets exactly the intended projects.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Scope can be defined explicitly or via portfolio/tags/filters; execution mode controls parallelism and ordering; scope is previewable before launch.
  • US-63: As an operator, I want child runs to be created per project using F-3 rules, so that each project executes correctly.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Each child run undergoes F-3 pre-flight readiness validation; blocked projects are reported without starting invalid runs.
  • US-64: As an operator, I want shared and overridden parameters, so that I can customize behavior per project where needed.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Shared parameters apply to all children; overrides are explicit, validated, and recorded; overrides do not mutate the parent group’s base definition.
  • US-65: As an operator, I want failure policy enforced across the group, so that group behavior is predictable when some children fail.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Policy options (e.g., continue, pause, halt, rollback where supported) are applied consistently; policy decisions are audited.
  • US-66: As an operator, I want aggregated monitoring and HIL gates for the group, so that I can manage all children centrally.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Group view shows per-child status, gate waits, failures, and progress; group-level gate decisions propagate or are handled per policy.
  • US-67: As an auditor, I want a complete parent-child audit trail, so that I can trace group execution end to end.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Audit links parent group, each child run, commands, gate decisions, and outcomes; records are immutable and queryable.

F-6: Manual Input and Operation Control

  • US-76: As an operator, I want to supply manual input to a live run or stage, so that I can unblock work that requires human-provided data.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Input is validated against the expected schema; input is attached to the correct run/stage; submission is auditable and idempotent where applicable.
  • US-77: As an operator, I want to decide HIL gates, so that runs can proceed past human-approval points.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Decisions include approve/reject/request-changes where supported; decision, actor, timestamp, and rationale are recorded; gate state updates consistently.
  • US-78: As an operator, I want to pause, resume, and cancel runs or stages, so that I can control execution interactively.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Commands respect current run/stage state; illegal transitions are rejected with clear errors; every command is auditable.
  • US-79: As an operator, I want to retry or skip stages, so that I can recover from failures or bypass non-critical work.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Retry/skip respects stage entry and policy constraints; skip requires authorization where configured; outcomes are recorded.
  • US-80: As an operator, I want to override parameters on a run or stage, so that I can correct configuration without restarting everything.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Overrides are validated, bounded by policy, applied only to the target scope, and recorded with before/after values.
  • US-81: As an operator, I want an emergency-wide halt, so that I can immediately stop unsafe or unwanted activity across the system.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Emergency halt stops new work and places active work into a safe state per policy; action requires appropriate role and is loudly audited.
  • US-82: As an operator, I want dry-run/preview and confirmation for manual actions, so that I can avoid destructive mistakes.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Supported commands provide a preview of effects; destructive or high-impact commands require explicit confirmation.
  • US-83: As an operator, I want idempotent, auditable commands with conflict handling, so that retries and concurrent actions are safe.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Commands support idempotency keys where applicable; optimistic concurrency detects conflicts; conflicts return actionable errors and do not silently overwrite state.
  • US-84: As a security administrator, I want manual actions gated by role, so that only authorized users can intervene.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Role/permission checks are enforced for every manual command; unauthorized attempts are denied and audited.

F-7: Auto Mode

  • US-91: As an operator, I want to choose and bind an autonomy level, so that Auto Mode operates within my risk tolerance.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Assist, supervised-auto, and full-auto levels are selectable; the bound policy records the chosen level and its scope.
  • US-92: As an operator, I want Auto Mode to decide HIL gates according to policy, so that runs can progress without manual gate handling.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Gate decisions are made only within policy bounds; decisions are explainable and recorded in the decision ledger.
  • US-93: As an operator, I want Auto Mode to auto-retry, auto-skip, auto-pause, auto-halt, and auto-escalate according to policy, so that failures are handled consistently.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Each automated action follows the configured policy; actions are idempotent where applicable; escalation occurs when policy is ambiguous.
  • US-94: As an operator, I want budget, time, and risk guardrails, so that Auto Mode cannot exceed safe operating limits.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Guardrails are evaluated before and during execution; breaches trigger the configured action (pause, halt, or escalate) and are audited.
  • US-95: As an operator, I want bounded automatic parameter overrides, so that minor issues can self-heal without unbounded changes.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Overrides are constrained to a policy-defined envelope; every override is recorded with reason and effect; out-of-bounds overrides are rejected.
  • US-96: As an auditor, I want an immutable, explainable decision ledger for Auto Mode, so that I can review why every autonomous decision was made.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Ledger records decision type, inputs, policy reference, outcome, timestamp, and correlation to run/stage; entries cannot be altered or deleted.
  • US-97: As an operator, I want a kill switch for immediate manual takeover, so that I can stop Auto Mode instantly.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Kill switch disables Auto Mode for the target scope immediately; manual control resumes per F-6 rules; activation is audited.
  • US-98: As an operator, I want Auto Mode to fail closed toward escalation, so that ambiguous situations do not cause unsafe autonomous actions.
  • Acceptance Criteria: When policy is missing, ambiguous, or contradictory, Auto Mode escalates rather than guessing; escalation is recorded and visible.

F-8: Dashboard UX Component System

  • US-106: As a dashboard user, I want a versioned design-token foundation, so that the UI is visually consistent across all surfaces.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Colors, spacing, typography, elevation, and motion are tokenized; tokens are versioned and consumed by components.
  • US-107: As a dashboard user, I want a primitive, composed, and feature component library, so that pipeline state is rendered consistently.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Components cover loading, empty, error, real-time, and interactive states; feature components compose primitives rather than redefining styles.
  • US-108: As a dashboard user, I want clear loading, empty, and error states, so that I always understand system status.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Every list/page defines loading (skeleton preferred), empty (with CTA where applicable), and error (inline/toast/recovery) states.
  • US-109: As a dashboard user, I want real-time updates, so that I see live pipeline changes without manual refresh.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Real-time updates reconcile with authoritative state; connection loss is indicated; stale data is not silently presented as live.
  • US-110: As a dashboard user, I want theming and responsive layouts, so that the UI works on my device and preferences.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Light/dark themes are supported; layouts adapt to desktop/tablet/mobile breakpoints; touch targets meet minimum sizes on mobile.
  • US-111: As an accessibility user, I want keyboard navigation, ARIA labels, sufficient contrast, and visible focus indicators, so that I can use the dashboard independently.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Interactive elements are keyboard reachable; contrast meets WCAG 2.1 AA; focus indicators are visible; screen-reader semantics are correct.
  • US-112: As an international user, I want locale-aware dates, times, numbers, and RTL readiness, so that the UI fits my language and region.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Date/time/number formatting follows user locale; RTL layouts are supported where required; language selector is available in settings.
  • US-113: As a motion-sensitive user, I want reduced-motion support, so that animations do not cause discomfort.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Motion respects prefers-reduced-motion; non-essential animations can be disabled; no flashing exceeds 3 Hz.

F-9: Global AI Chat Companion

  • US-121: As an operator, I want to open the AI companion from any dashboard surface, so that I can get help without leaving my current task.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Companion is available globally; opening it preserves current page context; it can be dismissed without losing work.
  • US-122: As an operator, I want the companion automatically bound to my current context, so that its answers are relevant.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Context includes project, run, stage, HIL gate, portfolio, multi-project run group, or auto-mode session where applicable; context is shown and can be adjusted.
  • US-123: As an operator, I want to ask questions about authoritative pipeline state, so that I can understand what is happening.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Answers read from published surfaces of F-1 through F-7; factual claims cite the authoritative object/source.
  • US-124: As an operator, I want the companion to explain behavior and diagnose failures, so that I can resolve issues faster.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Explanations reference logs, stage outcomes, gate states, and policies where available; uncertainty is stated rather than invented.
  • US-125: As an operator, I want the companion to draft commands for the manual command surface, so that I can act without it executing commands directly.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Drafted commands are handed to F-6 for preview and confirmation; the companion never executes commands itself.
  • US-126: As an operator, I want grounded answers with citations, so that I can trust the information.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Every factual statement about pipeline state includes a citation to the authoritative object; unsupported claims are refused or marked as unknown.
  • US-127: As an operator, I want the companion to refuse inventing state, commands, or exceeding my scope, so that it remains safe.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Companion refuses to invent state/commands or access data outside the operator’s authorization; refusal messages explain the limitation.
  • US-128: As an operator, I want graceful degradation to deterministic help and search when AI is unavailable, so that I can still get assistance.
  • Acceptance Criteria: When AI backend is unavailable, companion switches to help/search mode; deterministic help remains available; degradation is clearly indicated.

F-10: Voice Companion

  • US-136: As an operator, I want hands-free voice activation, so that I can talk to the companion without using the keyboard.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Voice activation starts microphone lifecycle with clear recording indicator; activation permissions are respected; deactivation stops capture.
  • US-137: As an operator, I want streaming speech-to-text, so that my speech is transcribed accurately and promptly.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Transcription streams partial results where supported; final transcript is available for review; errors are recoverable.
  • US-138: As an operator, I want spoken synthesis of grounded answers with citations, so that I can hear responses.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Answers are spoken using the resolved voice reference; citations are announced; synthesis failures degrade to text.
  • US-139: As an operator, I want barge-in, so that I can interrupt speech and continue the conversation naturally.
  • Acceptance Criteria: User speech interrupts playback; the system stops speaking and processes the new input; interruption is handled without losing context.
  • US-140: As an operator, I want hands-free HIL gate announcements and decisions, so that I can handle gates by voice.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Pending gates are announced with enough context; voice decisions are validated and routed to F-6 for confirmation/execution.
  • US-141: As an operator, I want voice-driven navigation, so that I can move around the dashboard hands-free.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Supported navigation commands are recognized; navigation respects authorization; the current context is announced.
  • US-142: As an operator, I want voice drafting of commands for the manual command surface, so that I can prepare actions by voice.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Voice-drafted commands are handed to F-6 for preview and confirmation; the voice layer never executes them directly.
  • US-143: As a multilingual operator, I want multilingual speech support, so that I can use the companion in my language.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Speech recognition and synthesis support configured languages/locales; language can be selected or inferred per policy.
  • US-144: As a privacy-conscious operator, I want transcript and audio privacy controls, so that my voice data is protected.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Operator can control retention/use of transcripts and audio; privacy settings are honored; data handling is auditable.
  • US-145: As an operator, I want graceful degradation to text, so that voice failures do not block me.
  • Acceptance Criteria: When microphone, STT, or TTS fails, the companion falls back to text input/output with a clear explanation.

F-11: Mobile Dashboard Companion

  • US-151: As a mobile operator, I want a secure, responsive view of pipeline state, so that I can monitor projects, runs, gates, and portfolios on the go.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Mobile view renders authoritative state from F-1 through F-7; layouts adapt to phone and tablet; access is authenticated and authorized.
  • US-152: As a mobile operator, I want notifications for gates, failures, Auto Mode decisions, and run completion, so that I can react promptly.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Notifications are configurable; deep links open the relevant object; critical alerts are distinguishable from informational ones.
  • US-153: As a mobile operator, I want read-first, action-safe behavior, so that I do not accidentally execute commands.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Mobile UI emphasizes review and monitoring; destructive actions require explicit confirmation or are routed to F-6 preview.
  • US-154: As a mobile operator, I want commands delegated to the manual command surface, so that every action is previewed, confirmed, and audited.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Any command initiated on mobile is handed to F-6 for preview/confirmation/execution; mobile never bypasses F-6 semantics.
  • US-155: As a mobile operator, I want offline read-only access to last-known state, so that I can review without connectivity.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Cached last-known state is available offline; stale data is clearly marked with last-updated time; commands are disabled offline.
  • US-156: As a mobile operator, I want graceful degradation when connectivity or AI services are unavailable, so that the app remains useful.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Offline/error banners appear; read-only cached data remains accessible; AI-dependent features fall back to deterministic mobile help.
  • US-157: As a security-conscious mobile operator, I want mobile-specific security, privacy, and accessibility, so that my data and device are protected.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Secure storage, session timeout, biometric/device auth where available, accessible touch targets, and screen-reader support are implemented per policy.

F-12: Test Management

  • US-166: As a test manager, I want to register and version test cases, so that tests are reusable and traceable.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Test case has definition, version, owner, assertions, and metadata; versions are immutable once published; history is auditable.
  • US-167: As a test manager, I want to compose test cases into suites and plans, so that I can organize and execute testing coherently.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Suites group cases; plans reference suites/cases with execution configuration; plan versions are immutable once published.
  • US-168: As a test manager, I want to bind test plans to pipeline stages and projects, so that tests run in the right context.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Bindings are explicit per project/stage; binding changes are auditable; unbound plans are clearly identifiable.
  • US-169: As a test manager, I want tests to run on demand, on schedule, or as a governed pipeline stage, so that testing fits the workflow.
  • Acceptance Criteria: All three execution modes are supported; stage-driven tests integrate with F-3 orchestration; execution triggers are recorded.
  • US-170: As a test manager, I want deterministic outcomes and evidence capture, so that results are reliable and reviewable.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Each test run records pass/fail/error/skipped, logs, artifacts, environment, and timestamps; evidence is immutable.
  • US-171: As a quality gate owner, I want plan-level quality gates that stage advancement consumes, so that pipelines do not advance on failed quality.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Gate verdict is computed from plan results per policy; F-3 consumes the verdict; gate outcome is published via stable API.
  • US-172: As a test manager, I want coverage tracking over pipeline artifacts, so that I can see what is tested and what is not.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Coverage is computed against configured artifact types; gaps are reported; coverage trends are available.
  • US-173: As a test manager, I want regression and flakiness management, so that unstable tests are identified and controlled.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Flaky tests are flagged based on repeat runs/history; regression sets can be curated; quarantine/waiver workflows are available per policy.
  • US-174: As a test manager, I want waiver and approval workflows, so that exceptions to quality gates are governed.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Waivers require authorized approval with reason and expiry; waivers are auditable; expired waivers no longer pass gates.
  • US-175: As an auditor, I want an immutable test audit trail, so that I can review every test definition, execution, and gate decision.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Audit records cover definitions, versions, runs, results, waivers, approvals, and gate verdicts; records are queryable and immutable.

F-13: API-First Access

  • US-181: As an API consumer, I want a versioned, documented API, so that I can integrate reliably over time.
  • Acceptance Criteria: API has explicit versions and lifecycle policy; documentation is machine-readable and published; deprecated versions follow policy.
  • US-182: As an API consumer, I want contract publication, so that I can generate clients and validate compatibility.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Contracts are published in a machine-readable format; contract changes are tracked; breaking changes follow versioning rules.
  • US-183: As an API consumer, I want authentication and scope-based authorization, so that access is controlled and least-privilege.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Clients authenticate per supported method; scopes map to capabilities; unauthorized requests are denied and audited.
  • US-184: As an API consumer, I want credential lifecycle management, so that I can rotate and revoke secrets safely.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Credentials can be created, rotated, revoked, and expired; lifecycle events are auditable; old credentials stop working after revocation.
  • US-185: As an API consumer, I want a uniform request/response envelope with pagination, filtering, and sparse fieldsets, so that responses are predictable and efficient.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Errors, pagination, filtering, and field selection follow consistent patterns; limits are enforced; responses are documented.
  • US-186: As an API consumer, I want idempotency and concurrency control, so that retries and concurrent updates are safe.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Idempotency keys are supported for unsafe operations; optimistic concurrency detects conflicts; conflict errors are actionable.
  • US-187: As an API consumer, I want asynchronous long-running-operation patterns, so that slow work can be managed without blocking.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Long-running operations return an operation ID; status can be polled or subscribed to; completion includes result/error.
  • US-188: As an API consumer, I want a streaming surface for live run telemetry, so that I can monitor runs in real time.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Streaming follows a documented protocol; reconnect/resume semantics are defined; streamed data matches authoritative state.
  • US-189: As an API consumer, I want webhooks and event subscriptions, so that I can react to changes without polling.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Subscriptions can be created and managed; events are delivered with retry/backoff; signatures/verification are supported.
  • US-190: As an API consumer, I want bulk operations and dry-run preview, so that I can act at scale safely.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Bulk endpoints accept multiple targets; dry-run returns predicted outcomes without side effects; per-item results are reported.
  • US-191: As an API consumer, I want rate limiting, quotas, and capability discovery, so that usage is predictable and discoverable.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Limits are documented and enforced; quota headers/errors are returned; capability discovery endpoint describes available operations.
  • US-192: As an API consumer, I want SDK/CLI generation and a sandbox environment, so that I can develop and test quickly.
  • Acceptance Criteria: SDKs/CLI are generated from contracts; sandbox supports safe experimentation; generated artifacts match the current version.
  • US-193: As an auditor, I want API activity auditing, so that I can trace API access and changes.
  • Acceptance Criteria: API calls are logged with actor, credential, scope, target, action, result, and correlation ID; logs are queryable and immutable per policy.

F-14: API Reuse and Extension Analysis

  • US-196: As an API governance analyst, I want an inventory snapshot of published API contracts, so that I know what capabilities already exist.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Snapshot includes contract versions, operations, schemas, and metadata; snapshot is read-only and timestamped.
  • US-197: As an API governance analyst, I want a demand registry, so that I can track requested or expected capabilities from consumers.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Demand entries include source, description, target area, priority, and status; registry is queryable and auditable.
  • US-198: As an API governance analyst, I want matching and gap detection between demand and contracts, so that I can find missing capabilities.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Matcher identifies exact matches, partial matches, and gaps; results include confidence and supporting references.
  • US-199: As an API governance analyst, I want reuse/extension classification, so that I can decide whether to reuse, extend additively, version, or introduce new API.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Each demand is classified with rationale; classification respects versioning and compatibility rules.
  • US-200: As an API governance analyst, I want duplication and overlap findings, so that I can reduce redundant API surface.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Findings identify overlapping operations/schemas; severity and recommended consolidation are provided.
  • US-201: As an API governance analyst, I want per-area coverage scores, so that I can prioritize governance work.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Coverage is scored by area against demand and contract inventory; scores are reproducible and trendable.
  • US-202: As an API governance analyst, I want review and decision records, so that reuse decisions are traceable.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Decision records include demand, analysis, outcome, approver, timestamp, and linked contract/version.
  • US-203: As an API governance analyst, I want published reuse guidance, so that teams can follow consistent API evolution rules.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Guidance is published per area; it references decisions and contracts; it is read-only against F-13 contracts.

F-15: Deployment Options

  • US-211: As a platform operator, I want a deployment-target catalog, so that I can choose where Product Forge runs.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Catalog lists supported targets, their guarantees, capabilities, and constraints; selection is recorded in a deployment record.
  • US-212: As a platform operator, I want topology and sizing profiles, so that I can match deployment capacity to expected load.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Profiles define topology, sizing, and scaling characteristics; chosen profile is part of the deployment manifest.
  • US-213: As a platform operator, I want provisioning and bootstrap paths per target, so that I can install reliably.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Each target has documented provisioning steps; bootstrap is repeatable and auditable; failures produce actionable diagnostics.
  • US-214: As a platform operator, I want configuration and secret management, so that deployments are secure and maintainable.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Configuration revisions are tracked; secrets are stored/retrieved per target’s secure mechanism; config changes are auditable.
  • US-215: As a platform operator, I want upgrade and rollback, so that I can maintain versions safely.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Upgrade plans are generated; rollback is available for supported targets; upgrade/rollback events are audited.
  • US-216: As a platform operator, I want scaling, high availability, and backup/restore, so that the platform is resilient.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Scaling policies are configurable; HA topology is supported per target; backup/restore procedures are documented and testable.
  • US-217: As a platform operator, I want observability, network/TLS, and identity wiring, so that operations are visible and secure.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Metrics/logs/traces can be wired to target observability; TLS and identity integration are configurable per target.
  • US-218: As a compliance officer, I want data-residency placement and air-gapped/offline delivery, so that regulatory and isolation requirements are met.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Data-residency placement is selectable where supported; air-gapped delivery path is documented; compliance-relevant settings are recorded.
  • US-219: As a platform operator, I want entitlement binding, drift detection, and multi-environment promotion, so that deployments stay compliant and consistent.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Entitlements are bound to deployments; drift from desired state is detected and reported; promotion between environments follows policy.
  • US-220: As a platform operator, I want hardening profiles and cost estimation, so that deployments are safe and affordable.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Hardening profiles can be applied; cost estimation is available per target/profile; estimates are recorded with the deployment plan.
  • US-221: As a platform operator, I want decommissioning, so that I can retire deployments cleanly.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Decommission plan covers data retention/export, secret revocation, resource teardown, and audit; execution is recorded.

F-16: Voice Reference Alignment

  • US-226: As a voice engineer, I want to register a target voice and locale, so that I can create a reference profile.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Target voice and locale are registered with metadata; duplicate/invalid registrations are rejected; registration is auditable.
  • US-227: As a voice engineer, I want to ingest source audio samples, so that I can build the reference from real speech.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Samples are accepted in supported formats; ingestion records source, duration, and quality metadata; rejected samples provide reasons.
  • US-228: As a voice engineer, I want preprocessing, normalization, phonetic/transcript alignment, and speaker embeddings, so that the reference is technically accurate.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Pipeline steps are executed in order; intermediate artifacts are retained per policy; failures identify the failing step.
  • US-229: As a voice engineer, I want objective alignment scoring and pass/review/fail verdicts, so that quality is measured consistently.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Scoring uses configured metrics/thresholds; verdict is recorded with evidence; review/fail outcomes block approval per policy.
  • US-230: As a voice engineer, I want immutable reference-profile versions, so that changes are traceable and reproducible.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Each profile version is immutable once published; version history links to alignment run and source samples.
  • US-231: As a voice engineer, I want approval and retirement workflows, so that reference profiles are governed.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Approval requires authorized role; retirement marks profile unavailable for new bindings; both actions are audited.
  • US-232: As a voice engineer, I want to bind profiles to languages/locales and Voice Companion resolution rules, so that the correct voice is used.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Bindings are explicit and versioned; F-10 resolves the correct profile per locale/context; fallback behavior is defined.
  • US-233: As a voice engineer, I want drift monitoring and cross-sample consistency checks, so that reference quality is maintained over time.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Drift checks compare new samples against the approved profile; inconsistencies are flagged; alerts/notifications are configurable.
  • US-234: As a voice engineer, I want search, import, and export of voice references, so that I can manage references efficiently.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Search covers metadata and quality attributes; import/export uses documented formats; access is role-gated.
  • US-235: As a security administrator, I want role-gated access and an immutable audit trail for voice references, so that sensitive voice data is controlled.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Roles gate registration, ingestion, scoring, approval, binding, and export; audit records cover all lifecycle actions.
  • US-236: As a voice engineer, I want settings/presets, batch alignment, failure diagnosis, preview synthesis, and completion notifications, so that I can operate efficiently at scale.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Presets are configurable; batch alignment reports per-item results; failures include diagnosis; preview synthesis uses the candidate profile; completion notifications are delivered per settings.

API Contracts

Scope: canonical HTTP/JSON contract for every feature F-1 … F-16. Semantics for each operation are owned by the feature named in the heading; other features reference these operations by name only and never redefine them.

Global Conventions (apply to every contract below)

Concern Rule
Base path /api/v1 (version segment is contractual; see F-13)
Media type application/json; charset=utf-8; text/event-stream for streaming
Auth Authorization: Bearer <token> or X-Api-Key: <key>; scope-checked per operation
Success envelope { "data": <resource|array>, "meta": { "requestId", "nextCursor?", "total?" } }
Error envelope { "error": { "code", "message", "details": [ { "field", "issue" } ], "requestId", "retryable" } }
Pagination Cursor-based: ?limit=1..200&cursor=<opaque> → meta.nextCursor
Filtering / sorting ?filter[status]=running&sort=-createdAt (allow-listed fields only)
Sparse fieldsets ?fields=id,name,status
Idempotency Idempotency-Key: <uuid> required on all state-changing POSTs; replay returns the original response with Idempotency-Replayed: true
Concurrency ETag on read; If-Match: <etag> required on PATCH/PUT; mismatch → 409 conflict.stale_revision
Dry run Dry-Run: true header on any command returns the predicted effect without persisting
Long-running ops 202 Accepted + Operation resource; poll GET /operations/{opId} or subscribe to its stream
Request correlation X-Request-Id echoed; meta.requestId always present
Rate limits X-RateLimit-Limit, X-RateLimit-Remaining, X-RateLimit-Reset; 429 with Retry-After

Shared error codes

Code HTTP Meaning
auth.unauthenticated 401 Missing/invalid credential
auth.forbidden 403 Authenticated but outside authorization scope
validation.field 422 Field-level validation failure (details[] populated)
not_found 404 Resource absent or not visible in caller scope
conflict.stale_revision 409 If-Match failed
conflict.invalid_state 409 Command illegal for current resource state
idempotency.mismatch 409 Same key, different payload
rate_limited 429 Quota exceeded
dependency.unavailable 503 Downstream subsystem down; retryable: true
internal 500 Unhandled; requestId required in support reports

F-1: Central Pipeline Control Plane

API-1 — Project lifecycle registry

Method Path Purpose
GET /projects List/filter/search projects (filter[status], filter[portfolioId], filter[owner], q)
POST /projects Create project (delegates full creation flow to F-2)
GET /projects/{projectId} Read one project
PATCH /projects/{projectId} Update mutable fields (name, description, owner, tags, portfolio membership)
POST /projects/{projectId}:archive Archive (non-destructive)
POST /projects/{projectId}:restore Restore archived project
DELETE /projects/{projectId} Permanent delete; requires ?confirm=<projectName>

Create request

{ "name": "string", "description": "string", "ownerId": "string",
  "portfolioId": "string|null", "tags": ["string"],
  "pipelineDefinitionId": "string", "pipelineVersionId": "string|null",
  "configurationRevision": "string|null" }

Project resource

{ "id": "prj_...", "name": "string", "description": "string",
  "ownerId": "string", "portfolioId": "string|null", "tags": [],
  "status": "active|archived", "pipelineBinding": { "definitionId", "versionId", "pinnedAt" },
  "configurationRevision": "rev_42", "createdAt": "ISO-8601", "updatedAt": "ISO-8601" }

Rules: name unique per owner, 3–80 chars; DELETE on a project with active runs → 409 conflict.invalid_state unless ?force=true and caller holds project:destroy. Permanent delete is asynchronous → 202 + Operation. Archived projects remain listed under filter[status]=archived. Concurrency enforced via configurationRevision exposed as ETag.

API-2 — Pipeline definitions and version pinning

Method Path Purpose
GET /pipeline-definitions List definitions
POST /pipeline-definitions Register definition
GET /pipeline-definitions/{definitionId} Definition with ordered stage list
GET /pipeline-definitions/{definitionId}/versions List immutable versions
POST /pipeline-definitions/{definitionId}/versions Publish new version
GET /pipeline-versions/{versionId} Read immutable version
POST /projects/{projectId}:upgrade-pipeline-version Explicit pin upgrade

Upgrade request { "targetVersionId": "pv_7", "effectiveFor": "new_runs_only" } Response 200 with updated pipelineBinding and configurationRevision. Rules: versions are immutable — any mutation returns 405; upgrade never mutates historical runs; effectiveFor currently only accepts new_runs_only (any other value → 422).

API-3 — Run execution control

Method Path Purpose
POST /projects/{projectId}/runs Start run (canonical initiation owned by F-3)
GET /runs List runs (filter[projectId], filter[status], filter[since])
GET /runs/{runId} Read run
POST /runs/{runId}:pause Pause
POST /runs/{runId}:resume Resume
POST /runs/{runId}:cancel Cancel ({ "reason": "string", "force": false })
POST /runs/{runId}:retry Retry ({ "fromStageId": "stg_3|null" })

Rules: every command carries Idempotency-Key; acknowledgement (202) is returned only after durable persistence; each command emits an audit record (POST /commands surface owned by F-6). Illegal transition (e.g. resume a cancelled run) → 409 conflict.invalid_state.

API-4 — Stage orchestration

Method Path Purpose
GET /runs/{runId}/stages Ordered stage instances with state, timings, outcome
GET /runs/{runId}/stages/{stageId} Single stage detail
GET /runs/{runId}/stages/{stageId}/log Paginated stage log
POST /runs/{runId}/stages/{stageId}:retry Retry one stage

Rules: stage order is derived from the pinned pipeline version and is read-only through the API; manual stage start is only permitted when the stage's entry conditions allow it, otherwise 409 conflict.invalid_state.

API-5 — Portfolio grouping and roll-up (registry-side view)

Method Path Purpose
GET /portfolios List portfolios (full resource owned by F-4)
GET /portfolios/{portfolioId}/rollup Aggregate state across member projects

Roll-up response

{ "portfolioId": "pf_1", "projectCount": 12,
  "runsByStatus": { "running": 3, "paused": 1, "failed": 2, "completed": 40 },
  "gateBacklog": 5, "failureCount24h": 2, "lastActivityAt": "ISO-8601",
  "computedAt": "ISO-8601", "stale": false }

Rules: read-only, cacheable (Cache-Control: max-age=15); stale: true when the execution subsystem is unreachable, with last known values retained.

F-2: Project Creation and Model Tier Management

API-1 — Project creation wizard (draft sessions)

Method Path Purpose
POST /project-drafts Start a resumable creation draft
GET /project-drafts/{draftId} Resume draft (returns current step + collected data)
PATCH /project-drafts/{draftId} Update one or more wizard steps
POST /project-drafts/{draftId}:validate Validate without committing
POST /project-drafts/{draftId}:submit Materialize the project (delegates to F-1 API-1)
DELETE /project-drafts/{draftId} Discard draft

Steps: idea → metadata → portfolio → pipeline → model_tier → review. PATCH request { "step": "idea", "values": { ... }, "revision": "rev_3" } Validate response { "valid": false, "items": [ { "step": "metadata", "field": "name", "issue": "duplicate" } ] } Resume response includes expiresAt (30 days) and completedSteps[]. Rules: drafts are private to the creator; submitting twice with the same Idempotency-Key returns the same project; submit without a valid idea → 422 validation.field.

API-2 — Idea capture

Method Path Purpose
PATCH /project-drafts/{draftId} (step: "idea") Set seed idea text and optional structured fields

Payload { "ideaText": "string (<=20000 chars)", "goal": "string|null", "targetUsers": "string|null", "successSignals": ["string"] } Rules: ideaText required at submit; structured fields optional and preserved verbatim as the seed artifact.

API-3 — Model tier catalog and custom tiers

Method Path Purpose
GET /model-tiers List tiers (predefined + custom)
POST /model-tiers Create custom tier from catalog models
GET /model-tiers/{tierId} Read tier
PATCH /model-tiers/{tierId} Update custom tier (predefined → 405)
DELETE /model-tiers/{tierId} Delete custom tier (blocked if bound to a project)
POST /model-tiers:validate Validate a proposed tier composition
GET /model-providers List providers
GET /model-providers/{providerId}/models List models with capability/cost metadata

Tier resource

{ "id": "tier_balanced", "kind": "predefined|custom", "name": "balanced",
  "roles": { "planner": { "providerId", "modelId", "params": {} },
             "worker": { ... }, "reviewer": { ... } },
  "constraints": { "maxCostPerRun": 25.0, "maxTokensPerRun": 5000000 },
  "revision": "rev_2", "createdBy": "usr_1", "createdAt": "ISO-8601" }

Rules: a tier must define every role the pipeline version requires; unknown modelId → 422; deleting a tier bound to a project → 409 conflict.invalid_state; predefined tier edits rejected with 405.

API-4 — Tier selection at creation

Method Path Purpose
PATCH /project-drafts/{draftId} (step: "model_tier") Bind tier choice or inline tier definition

Payload { "tierId": "tier_balanced" } or { "inlineTier": { ...same shape as POST /model-tiers... } } Rules: inline tiers are persisted as custom tiers on submit; unresolved tier blocks submit with 422.

F-3: E2E Pipeline Initiation and Monitoring

API-1 — Run initiation

POST /projects/{projectId}/runs

Request

{ "pipelineVersionId": "pv_7", "label": "nightly", "startAtStageId": "stg_2|null",
  "parameters": { "key": "value" }, "idempotencyKey": "uuid",
  "autoModePolicyId": "amp_1|null" }

Response 202

{ "data": { "runId": "run_9f2", "status": "queued", "operationId": "op_55" },
  "meta": { "requestId": "req_..." } }

Rules: startAtStageId only honored when that stage's entry conditions permit manual start, else 422 validation.field; pipelineVersionId defaults to the project's pinned version; replayed idempotencyKey returns the original runId.

API-2 — Pre-flight readiness validation

POST /projects/{projectId}/runs:preflight → 200

{ "ready": false,
  "checks": [
    { "id": "seed_idea", "status": "pass" },
    { "id": "metadata", "status": "pass" },
    { "id": "model_tier", "status": "fail", "remedy": "Bind a model tier to the project" },
    { "id": "pipeline_binding", "status": "pass" },
    { "id": "gate_policy", "status": "fail", "remedy": "Set an HIL gate policy" }
  ] }

Rules: no stage executes before all checks pass; initiation with a failing report returns 409 conflict.invalid_state carrying the same checks[].

API-3 — Run plan materialization

GET /runs/{runId}/plan → ordered materialized stages with resolved parameters, resolved tier roles, and gate placements. Read-only; reflects the pinned version snapshot at initiation.

API-4 — Live progress and telemetry streaming

GET /runs/{runId}/events (Accept: text/event-stream)

Event types: run.status, stage.started, stage.progress, stage.succeeded, stage.failed, stage.retried, gate.opened, gate.decided, log.append, heartbeat.

event: stage.progress
data: {"runId":"run_9f2","stageId":"stg_4","percent":62,"message":"generating","ts":"ISO-8601"}

Rules: Last-Event-ID resumes without gaps; heartbeat every 15 s; the client must tolerate out-of-order delivery across stageIds; stream closes with run.status=terminal plus a final run.summary event.

API-5 — Run detail and stage outcomes

GET /runs/{runId} → run header (status, project, pinned version, timings, counters, terminal reason). GET /runs/{runId}/stages → per-stage { stageId, name, ordinal, status, startedAt, endedAt, attempt, outcome: {code, summary}, gateId|null }.

API-6 — HIL gate inspection

GET /runs/{runId}/gates → gate instances (opened, decided, expired) with the payload the operator must review. GET /gates/{gateId} → single gate with available decision options and expiry.

Rules: gate decisions are not made here — see F-6 API-3. Expiry policy and options come from the pinned version's gate policy.

API-7 — Run audit and timeline

GET /runs/{runId}/audit?cursor= → immutable, chronological events (initiated, stage transitions, commands, gate decisions, overrides), each with actor, timestamp, and correlation id. Read-only; no mutation endpoint exists.

F-4: Portfolio Management

API-1 — Portfolio registry and lifecycle

Method Path Purpose
GET /portfolios List (filter[owner], q)
POST /portfolios Create
GET /portfolios/{portfolioId} Read
PATCH /portfolios/{portfolioId} Update name/description/owner/labels
POST /portfolios/{portfolioId}:archive Archive
POST /portfolios/{portfolioId}:restore Restore
DELETE /portfolios/{portfolioId} Delete (empty portfolios only, else 409)

API-2 — Membership control

Method Path Purpose
GET /portfolios/{portfolioId}/projects List members
POST /portfolios/{portfolioId}/projects Add members { "projectIds": ["prj_1","prj_2"] }
DELETE /portfolios/{portfolioId}/projects/{projectId} Remove member

Rules: a project belongs to at most one portfolio — adding a project already in another portfolio returns 409 conflict.invalid_state with the conflicting portfolioId; membership writes echo the affected project's new configurationRevision.

API-3 — Aggregated roll-up

GET /portfolios/{portfolioId}/rollup?window=24h → run counts by status, gate backlog with oldest age, failure counts, capacity pressure, last activity, per-project breakdown array, computedAt, stale. GET /portfolios/{portfolioId}/activity?cursor= → merged activity feed across members (run.started, stage.failed, gate.opened, gate.decided).

Rules: roll-ups are computed from authoritative F-1/F-3 state; a read must never block on the execution subsystem — unreachable dependencies degrade to last-known values with stale: true and 503 only when no snapshot exists.

API-4 — Scoped bulk operations

POST /portfolios/{portfolioId}/runs:bulk-start POST /portfolios/{portfolioId}/runs:bulk-pause POST /portfolios/{portfolioId}/runs:bulk-cancel POST /portfolios/{portfolioId}/runs:bulk-retry-failed

Request { "projectIds": ["prj_1"], "excludeProjectIds": [], "filter": { "status": ["failed"] } } Response 202 + Operation with per-project child result links. Rules: every child run is created under F-3's initiation and pre-flight rules; a project failing pre-flight is skipped (not failed) and reported in operation.results[]; dry run via Dry-Run: true returns the resolved target list without executing.

API-5 — Portfolio authorization scope

GET /portfolios/{portfolioId}/access → caller's effective scopes on the portfolio and derived project scope. GET /portfolios/{portfolioId}/members/roles → role assignments (portfolio-scoped, does not widen F-1 project scope).

API-6 — Portfolio reporting

GET /portfolios/{portfolioId}/reports/{reportKind} (reportKind ∈ throughput | failures | gate-aging | capacity) POST /portfolios/{portfolioId}/reports:export → 202 + downloadable artifact reference (format: json|csv).

F-5: Multi-Project Runs

API-1 — Run group lifecycle

Method Path Purpose
POST /run-groups Create parent run group
GET /run-groups List (filter[portfolioId], filter[status])
GET /run-groups/{groupId} Read parent with aggregate counters
PATCH /run-groups/{groupId} Update name/labels (not scope once started)
DELETE /run-groups/{groupId} Delete group record (children retained)

Create request

{ "name": "Q3 regression sweep", "ownerId": "usr_1", "portfolioId": "pf_1|null",
  "scope": { "kind": "explicit|portfolio|filter",
             "projectIds": ["prj_1"], "filter": { "tags": ["payments"] } },
  "pipelineVersionId": "pv_7", "executionMode": "parallel|sequential|canary",
  "maxConcurrency": 5, "failurePolicy": "continue|halt_group|halt_project",
  "sharedParameters": { "k": "v" },
  "projectOverrides": [ { "projectId": "prj_2", "parameters": { "k": "v2" } } ] }

Rules: scope must resolve to ≥ 1 project else 422; maxConcurrency 1–100; failurePolicy=halt_group cancels queued children only, never in-flight ones; group creation is durable and resumable after platform restart.

API-2 — Child runs

Method Path Purpose
GET /run-groups/{groupId}/children Child runs with resolved parameters and status
GET /run-groups/{groupId}/children/{childRunId} Single child detail
POST /run-groups/{groupId}:retry-failed Retry only failed/cancelled children

Rules: each child is created through F-3 API-1 and inherits its pre-flight, plan, and audit rules; sharedParameters are merged with projectOverrides (override wins) and the merged set is recorded per child.

API-3 — Group control

Method Path Purpose
POST /run-groups/{groupId}:pause Pause dispatch (in-flight children complete)
POST /run-groups/{groupId}:resume Resume dispatch
POST /run-groups/{groupId}:cancel Cancel ({ "includeChildren": true, "force": false })
POST /run-groups/{groupId}:escalate Escalate to operator queue

API-4 — Aggregated monitoring, gates, streaming

GET /run-groups/{groupId}/rollup → children by status, stage-level funnel, failure clusters, gate backlog. GET /run-groups/{groupId}/gates → cross-project gate queue, each entry linking its owning child run. GET /run-groups/{groupId}/events (text/event-stream) → group.status, child.started, child.succeeded, child.failed, gate.opened (events carry both groupId and childRunId). Rules: gate decisions are made per child through F-6 API-3 — the group view never decides a gate on the operator's behalf.

API-5 — Parent-child audit

GET /run-groups/{groupId}/audit → single immutable trail linking parent commands to child run ids and to the downstream F-3 audit records.

F-6: Manual Input and Operation Control

API-1 — Uniform command envelope

POST /commands

{ "target": { "kind": "run|stage|group|system", "id": "run_9f2" },
  "action": "pause|resume|cancel|retry|skip|override_parameters|provide_input|decide_gate|emergency_halt",
  "arguments": { "reason": "string", "fromStageId": "stg_3" },
  "dryRun": false, "expectedRevision": "rev_42" }

Response 202 { "data": { "commandId": "cmd_1", "accepted": true, "operationId": "op_9" } } Rules: Idempotency-Key mandatory; every command is persisted before acknowledgement and produces exactly one audit entry; expectedRevision mismatch → 409 conflict.stale_revision; unsupported action for target.kind → 422; unauthorized actions → 403 auth.forbidden.

API-2 — Preview / dry run

POST /commands:preview → same body as API-1, returns

{ "effect": { "willTransitionTo": "paused", "affectedRuns": ["run_9f2"],
              "warnings": ["stage stg_4 will complete before pausing"] },
  "requiresConfirmation": true }

Rules: preview never persists; any command marked requiresConfirmation must be re-issued with confirm: true or it is rejected with 409 conflict.invalid_state.

API-3 — HIL gate decisions

POST /gates/{gateId}/decisions

{ "decision": "approve|reject|request_changes|delegate",
  "rationale": "string (required for reject/request_changes, >=10 chars)",
  "overrides": { "key": "value" }, "delegateToUserId": "usr_7|null" }

Rules: gate must be opened and unexpired (409 otherwise); decision recorded immutably with actor, rationale, and inputs reviewed; delegate transfers the decision right without executing it.

API-4 — Manual input and parameter overrides

POST /runs/{runId}/stages/{stageId}:provide-input → { "input": {...}, "reason": "string" } POST /runs/{runId}/stages/{stageId}:override-parameters → { "parameters": {...}, "scope": "stage|remaining_stages", "reason": "string" } POST /runs/{runId}/stages/{stageId}:skip → { "reason": "string", "acknowledgeDownstreamImpact": true }

Rules: overrides are bounded — values outside the stage's declared parameter schema → 422; overrides never rewrite historical stage records, only future ones; skipping a gate-bearing stage requires the skip to be policy-permitted, else 403.

API-5 — Conflict handling

Any command issued against a resource whose state changed since the caller's last read returns 409 with { "error": { "code": "conflict.stale_revision" }, "current": { "status": "...", "revision": "rev_43" } } Rules: clients must re-read and re-preview; the API never silently applies a stale command.

API-6 — Emergency-wide halt

POST /system:emergency-halt → { "scope": "global|portfolio|project", "scopeId": "pf_1|null", "reason": "string" } POST /system:emergency-halt:clear → { "reason": "string" } Rules: requires the dedicated system:halt role; halts dispatch immediately while letting in-flight stages reach a safe checkpoint; halt and clear are both idempotent and recorded in the global audit; no other command is accepted for affected targets while halted (409 conflict.invalid_state).

API-7 — Command history and audit

GET /commands?filter[targetId]=&filter[actorId]=&filter[action]=&cursor= → paginated command log. GET /commands/{commandId} → status (accepted|applied|rejected|superseded), actor, before/after summary, linked operation. Rules: read-only; entries are immutable for the life of the installation.

F-7: Auto Mode

API-1 — Policy registry and versioning

Method Path Purpose
GET /auto-mode/policies List policies
POST /auto-mode/policies Create
GET /auto-mode/policies/{policyId} Read
PATCH /auto-mode/policies/{policyId} Update (draft revision)
POST /auto-mode/policies/{policyId}/versions Publish immutable version
GET /auto-mode/policies/{policyId}/versions List versions
POST /auto-mode/policies/{policyId}/versions/{versionId}:activate Activate
POST /auto-mode/policies/{policyId}/versions/{versionId}:retire Retire

Policy body

{ "autonomyLevel": "assist|supervised_auto|full_auto",
  "gateRules": [ { "gateKind": "quality|approval", "when": { "risk": "low" },
                   "decision": "approve|reject|escalate" } ],
  "retryPolicy": { "maxAttempts": 3, "backoff": "exponential", "retryOn": ["transient"] },
  "guardrails": { "maxCostPerRun": 50.0, "maxWallClockMinutes": 240,
                  "maxConsecutiveFailures": 5, "riskCeiling": "medium" },
  "overrideBounds": { "allowedKeys": ["temperature"], "maxDelta": 0.2 },
  "escalation": { "channel": "operator_queue", "onAmbiguity": "escalate" } }

Rules: assist may never approve a gate without a human; an activated policy is immutable; ambiguity is never resolved by inference — the platform escalates (onAmbiguity default escalate).

API-2 — Policy binding to project or run

PUT /projects/{projectId}/auto-mode → { "policyVersionId": "amp_1:v3" } DELETE /projects/{projectId}/auto-mode → unbind (falls back to manual operation) POST /runs/{runId}/auto-mode:enable → { "policyVersionId": "amp_1:v3", "applyFrom": "next_stage|immediate" } POST /runs/{runId}/auto-mode:disable → kill switch; { "reason": "string" } Rules: the kill switch takes effect within one stage boundary, stops all further automatic decisions, and returns control to F-6 without reverting already-applied decisions; binding always requires an explicit published version (a moving "latest" pointer is rejected with 422).

API-3 — Decision ledger

GET /runs/{runId}/auto-mode/decisions?cursor= → immutable entries

{ "decisionId": "amd_1", "at": "ISO-8601", "policyVersionId": "amp_1:v3",
  "ruleId": "gateRules[0]", "target": { "kind": "gate", "id": "gate_7" },
  "decision": "approve", "rationale": "risk=low, policy rule matched",
  "inputs": { "risk": "low" }, "guardrailsConsulted": ["maxCostPerRun"],
  "escalated": false }

GET /auto-mode/decisions/{decisionId} → full explainability record including the policy snapshot evaluated. Rules: every automatic action has exactly one ledger entry; entries are append-only and cannot be edited or deleted.

API-4 — Guardrail status and breaches

GET /runs/{runId}/auto-mode/guardrails → current consumption vs. limits, remaining budget/time, breach state. GET /auto-mode/breaches?filter[runId]= → every breach with the action taken (pause|halt|escalate). Rules: a breach always produces an escalation or halt, never a silent continuation.

API-5 — Escalation queue

GET /auto-mode/escalations?filter[status]=open → items handed back to a human, linked to the run/stage/gate and the ledger entry that raised them. POST /auto-mode/escalations/{escalationId}:claim → claim for handling (routes into F-6 command surface).

F-8: Dashboard UX Component System

API-1 — Design tokens

GET /ux/design-tokens?version=latest → versioned token document (colour, spacing, typography, elevation, motion, breakpoints, z-index) plus tokenVersion and deprecations[]. Rules: consumers must pin a tokenVersion; tokens are additive-only within a major version.
